ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-16-CD150416.u172	With venial, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, as the Minister has pointed out, we are faced with a reform that places its focus especially on two aspects that require permanent attention from public institutions and that are of great benefit to citizens: the modernisation of justice and the fight against gender-based violence. The first of these objectives, the modernization and transformation of justice, as you know, is not new. For more than a decade there has been a demand for justice that acts quickly, effectively and quality, in which response times are reasonable and the maximum possible levels of professionalization and specialization are achieved. The bill that the Government has referred to the Courts does precisely that, undertake a number of necessary reforms in the Organic Law of the Judiciary to provide an adequate response to the general demand for a more efficient functioning of the Administration of Justice, so that citizens receive a service that not only provides them with solutions but also takes place within a reasonable time. In short, the measures announced to us by the Minister should be welcomed if our challenge, that of all, is to ensure that justice works properly. Nevertheless, this afternoon we are debating five amendments in their entirety; amendments by which the groups that have subscribed to them defend an initial veto of this draft calling for its return; amendments by which they refuse to allow our obsolete judicial organisation to be made more flexible, with which they criticize the greater specialization in our courts and amendments with which they oppose a new system of burden-sharing. Frankly, I must tell you that I do not understand the reasons why you are opposed to this reform. I cannot understand that you reject a reform that will serve not only to modernize and speed up our justice, but also to improve the fight against gender-based violence, which is the other great objective of the project we are debating today. Ladies and gentlemen, some may think that it is better to do nothing or that the current system works reasonably well. To those who think so, I am sorry to say that citizens do not share that view. I shall begin by referring to the amendments tabled by Convergence i Unió and the Basque Parliamentary Group, because they are amendments which present common points. Firstly, with regard to the establishment of judicial councils in the autonomous communities, I would like to point out to you that their possible regulation in the area of the judiciary is a legislative option, rather than an obligation, as is apparent from the judgement of the Constitutional Court 31/2010. This is therefore a legitimate proposal which is not, however, required by society as a whole. Furthermore, article 122 of the Constitution links the status of a governing body with the principle of judicial unity. This is a reality already interpreted by the Constitutional Court, which in June 2010 declared unconstitutional the Council of Justice of Catalonia regulated in its Statute of Autonomy. Therefore, the autonomous justice councils, outside the competences assumed by the autonomous communities in relation to the administration of justice, cannot be governing bodies of the judiciary, which seems to be what you intend, and therefore cannot have competence in the matter of appointments, promotions, inspection or disciplinary regime, for the simple reason that no other body, except the General Council of the Judiciary, can exercise the function of governing the jurisdictional bodies integrated in the judiciary. With regard to the fact that knowledge of the languages proper to the Autonomous Communities is a requirement or only a merit, I do not want to reopen a debate that we have already had in this House, so I will confine myself to pointing out that this proposal shows little sensitivity towards the model of linguistic co-officiality. Under the imposition of the exclusive and exclusive requirement of the own language and co-official of the corresponding autonomous communities for judges, judges, prosecutors and by extension to all professionals involved in judicial proceedings, you seek to deny the legitimate rights of those who freely opt for the use of the Castilian. To try to reconvert the present plural system, where the knowledge of such languages is considered as preferential merit but not as a requirement in the competitions for jurisdictional bodies in these autonomous communities, in order to make it a strict form of monolingualism in the judicial as rigid and absolute as the one they present, is - you know - not only limiting and impoverishing but incompatible with the constitutional design. With regard to their criticism of the composition of the Standing Committee of the General Council of the Judiciary, I would like to inform you that, following the experience of almost a year of operation of the new Council, the bill increases its membership from five to seven members, as proposed by the General Council of the Judiciary in its report to the preliminary draft of the Organic Law. Moreover, the model of the council regulated by Organic Law 4/2013 is maintained, which, beyond political criticism, responds to the democratic decision of the General Courts, whose will is respected in the text we are discussing. I now turn to the amendment tabled by the Plural Left, which states that justice needs a change of organizational model, but not this one. I think we should not worry, what would not be natural and should even make us think - both formations, of course - is that you liked this project very much. Coming now to your criticisms, you come to consider that reform is a conglomerate of measures of little silence, but you have not been able to propose different or alternative measures, neither large nor small scale, that could improve the legislative text that is under way. Among its criticisms come to stress that the model of reform remains in all its ineffectiveness, since the courts of instance are left aside. At this point you will not be surprised that the first feeling that I have read such a statement is of profound perplexity, for the truth is that they have changed and a lot of opinion in recent months. I do not think I have to remind you that with the creation of provincial courts of instance in all the jurisdictional orders provided for in the preliminary draft it was intended to replace the current judicial parties, and you radically opposed that proposal. The reason why the bill has finally opted for the maintenance of judicial parties, abandoning the model of courts of instance, has been in the Government's interest that judicial parties remain a factor of territorial cohesion, and I believe that this should not be criticized by its group. ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-16-CD150416.u179	Next, the secretary will proceed to read an institutional statement from all groups on the future network of Cherenkov telescopes [[CTA]]
ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-16-CD150416.u180	With venial, Mr. President. The future network of Cherenkov telescopes will be the largest gamma-ray observatory in the world and will allow mapping of much of the previously unknown territory. It is a great international facility that will include two locations; one in the northern hemisphere, and the other in the south, to cover the whole sky. A few days ago, the thirteen countries financing the project announced that they will start negotiations with Spain and Mexico to decide which of the two will host the northern observatory. The CTA will cost around €200 million and is expected to be completed around 2020. It will be ten times more powerful than the current Cherenkov telescopes and among its main objectives is to reveal the origin of cosmic rays and explore dark matter. Overall, some 1,500 scientists and engineers from 29 countries participate in the project. Getting to house an installation like this would have no precedent in Spain. The proposed place is the Roque de los Muchachos Observatory, on the island of La Palma, where there are already front-line telescopes such as the Gran Telescopio de Canarias. The Government of the State, the Government of the Canary Islands and the Institute of Astrophysics of the Canary Islands have been working for months to promote a candidacy that would mean, in the event of being realized, the consolidation of the Canary Islands as one of the most important spaces on the planet in the study and research of astronomy. The final decision on the site will be taken before December. For this reason, the Congress of Deputies expresses its support for the Government of the State, in collaboration with the Government of the Canary Islands and the Institute of Astrophysics of the Canary Islands, to continue to make progress in the open negotiations to ensure that the Roque de los Muchachos, on the island of La Palma, is the seat of the future Cherenkov Telescope Network.
